		<item>
		<title>Enhancing Microsoft Azure Virtual Machine lifecycle</title>
		<link>https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/enhancing-microsoft-azure-virtual-machine-lifecycle/</link>
					<comments>https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/enhancing-microsoft-azure-virtual-machine-lifecycle/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[The Microsoft Azure Team]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 28 Sep 2026 15:00:00 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Compute]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Management and governance]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/?p=54069</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Our Virtual Machine Lifecycle policy guides how we manage these transitions, giving Azure customers transparency, predictability, and guidance.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/enhancing-microsoft-azure-virtual-machine-lifecycle/">Enhancing Microsoft Azure Virtual Machine lifecycle</a> appeared first on <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog">Microsoft Azure Blog</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[
<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Azure continuously advances through new infrastructure generations, platform capabilities, services, and innovations that lead the industry in performance, security, reliability, and efficiency. Our Virtual Machine (VM) Lifecycle policy guides how we manage these transitions, giving Azure customers transparency, predictability, and guidance to plan for and execute technology changes with confidence. Our goal is to help every organization plan and navigate these modernization steps in line with Azure&#8217;s latest innovations and ongoing platform investments.</p>



<div class="wp-block-buttons is-content-justification-center is-layout-flex wp-container-core-buttons-is-layout-a89b3969 wp-block-buttons-is-layout-flex">
<div class="wp-block-button"><a class="wp-block-button__link wp-element-button" href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/virtual-machines/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Learn more about virtual machines in Azure</a></div>
</div>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">To give you access to the most advanced cloud and AI infrastructure, deployed on the most sophisticated silicon and hardware, the Azure Virtual Machine lifecycle is built around three customer outcomes:</p>



<ul class="wp-block-list">
<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Transparency</strong> to easily understand the lifecycle status of Azure technologies, Microsoft&#8217;s level of ongoing investment, and the recommended path forward as new innovations become available.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Predictability</strong> to plan against defined lifecycle stages, understand what to expect at each stage, and advance notice as technologies progress through their lifecycle.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Guidance </strong>to help you modernize and adopt newer technologies rapidly and with confidence, along with AI-led tooling to accelerate workload transitions and recommendations.</li>
</ul>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">This lifecycle provides a shared taxonomy, common stages, and consistent commitments for how we communicate technology evolution.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/virtual-machines/sizes/overview?tabs=breakdownseries%2Cgeneralsizelist%2Ccomputesizelist%2Cmemorysizelist%2Cstoragesizelist%2Cgpusizelist%2Cfpgasizelist%2Chpcsizelist" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Azure General Purpose, Memory Optimized, Compute Optimized, and Storage Optimized VMs</a> are the first VM families covered by this policy.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The lifecycle stages, spanning <strong><em>Current</em>, <em>Extended</em>, <em>End of Life</em>,</strong> and <strong><em>Retired,</em></strong> are defined below, with dedicated Microsoft investment levels, regional and quota availability, applicable purchasing options, and pricing. The policy establishes clear lifecycle stages, expected customer experiences, and Microsoft&#8217;s commitments at each stage so customers can confidently plan, deploy, and modernize workloads over time.</p>



<figure class="wp-block-table aligncenter"><table class="has-fixed-layout"><tbody><tr><td class="has-text-align-center" data-align="center" colspan="2"><strong>Azure Virtual Machine Lifecycle Policy</strong></td></tr><tr><td><strong>Stage</strong></td><td><strong>Description</strong></td></tr><tr><td><strong>Current</strong></td><td><strong>Customer recommendation</strong>: Customers can confidently deploy new workloads, expand existing deployments, and optimize their Azure investments using VMs in the <em>Current</em> stage of the lifecycle. These VMs are specifically recommended for new deployments. <br><strong>Microsoft investment</strong>: During this stage, products receive standard maintenance and support.<br><strong>Availability</strong>:<em> Current</em> VM series are generally available to customers and have broad availability across Azure regions globally.<br><strong>Purchasing</strong> <strong>options and pricing</strong>: Pay-as-you-go pricing and Azure Savings Plans. Azure Reserved VM Instances may also be offered.  </td></tr><tr><td><strong>Extended</strong></td><td><strong>Customer recommendation</strong>: Azure VMs in the <em>Extended</em> stage remain supported for existing workloads; however, customers should begin evaluating and planning a transition to VMs in the <em>Current</em> stage.<br><strong>Microsoft investment</strong>: During this stage, products continue to receive standard maintenance and support.<br><strong>Availability</strong>: VMs in the <em>Extended</em> stage remain generally available across multiple regions. However, VMs in this stage may be subject to quota restrictions. <strong>Purchasing options and pricing</strong>: Pay-as-you-go pricing and Azure Savings Plans. Azure Reserved VM Instances may also be offered. Changes to purchasing options and pricing are possible.</td></tr><tr><td><strong>End of Life</strong></td><td><strong>Customer recommendation:</strong> VMs enter the <em>End of Life</em> stage when their retirement is formally announced. These VMs are based on older platform generations and do not benefit from the latest advancements in performance, security, reliability, and efficiency. Customers should actively plan and transition the workloads to VMs in the <em>Current</em> or <em>Extended</em> stages.<br><strong>Microsoft investment:</strong> During this stage, products continue to receive standard maintenance and support. In addition, Azure will provide notifications, migration tooling, and guidance to help customers move to newer versions.<br><strong>Availability:</strong> VMs in the <em>End of Life</em> stage remain available to existing customers in currently supported regions and within their existing quota allocations, subject to available capacity. New deployments and quota requests are subject to restriction. Microsoft will deploy new workload placement capabilities for VMs in the <em>End of Life</em> stage. While customers may experience minor workload performance variations in rare scenarios, existing Azure Virtual Machines SLAs will be met. These capabilities allow Microsoft to improve capacity availability for VMs in the <em>End of Life</em> stage as well as help support continued investments in modern hardware, new capabilities, and global scale.<br><strong>Purchasing options and pricing</strong>: Pay-as-you-go pricing and Azure Savings Plans remain available, while Reserved Instances may no longer be offered. When VMs enter the <em>End of Life</em> stage, customers should expect changes to pricing and availability.</td></tr><tr><td><strong>Retired</strong></td><td><strong>Customer recommendation:</strong> <em>Retired</em> VMs are no longer available and can no longer be provisioned. Customer modernization should be complete, with workloads now deployed to VMs in the <em>Current</em> or <em>Extended</em> stages.<br><strong>Microsoft investment:</strong> VMs in a <em>Retired</em> series are no longer covered by a service level agreement (SLA) and are not eligible for Microsoft support.<br><strong>Availability:</strong> New VM creation is no longer available, and existing VMs will be deallocated.<br><strong>Purchasing options and pricing:</strong> VMs in a retired series are no longer available for purchase.</td></tr></tbody></table></figure>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong><em>Note</em></strong><em>: Lifecycle details described in this policy represent general guidance and may be subject to product-specific, regional, or operational exceptions.</em></p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="platform-experiences-and-tools-to-help-simplify-transitions">Platform experiences and tools to help simplify transitions</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Microsoft is committed to helping you modernize your workload portfolio with the greatest possible speed, confidence, and simplicity. Azure Advisor and Azure Service Health give you clear insight into your subscriptions and resources—helping you discover upcoming VM retirements, identify affected resources, and track your modernization progress with recommended actions built in. Beyond this, Microsoft is investing in intelligent, AI-augmented tools designed to apply lifecycle guidance across your entire workload portfolio, further reducing friction as you modernize.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Microsoft is committed to helping you navigate these infrastructure transitions and take advantage of the next wave of innovation. As Azure continues to advance, our commitment is to make change predictable, transparent, and supported through clear lifecycle guidance, early visibility, and practical help to modernize with confidence. This policy is an important step toward making modernization easier to plan, simpler to execute, and more valuable for every Azure customer.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">For more information about the Azure Virtual Machines lifecycle, visit the <a href="https://aka.ms/VM-lifecycle/learn/vm-lifecycle-policy" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">VM lifecycle overview page</a>.</p>

<div class="promotional promotional--has-media promotional--media-right">
	<div class="promotional__wrapper">

			<div class="promotional__content">
				

<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="azure-virtual-machines">Azure Virtual Machines</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Create and manage virtual machines </p>



<div class="wp-block-buttons is-layout-flex wp-block-buttons-is-layout-flex">

		<item>
		<title>Ship agents faster with expanded model choice, voice agents, and continuous optimization</title>
		<link>https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/ship-agents-faster-with-expanded-model-choice-voice-agents-and-continuous-optimization/</link>
					<comments>https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/ship-agents-faster-with-expanded-model-choice-voice-agents-and-continuous-optimization/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Tina Schuchman]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 24 Sep 2026 18:00:00 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[AI + machine learning]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Management and governance]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/?p=54013</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>The best model for your business will keep changing. Adopting it should move your business forward, not send your team back to rebuild the architecture around it.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/ship-agents-faster-with-expanded-model-choice-voice-agents-and-continuous-optimization/">Ship agents faster with expanded model choice, voice agents, and continuous optimization</a> appeared first on <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog">Microsoft Azure Blog</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[
<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The best model for your business will keep changing. Adopting it should move your business forward, not send your team back to rebuild the architecture around it. As models advance, organizations need the freedom to choose the right model for each workload, and an agent foundation that can evolve with those choices.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/products/ai-foundry" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">Microsoft Foundry</a> provides that model—and harness—agnostic foundation for building and running agents. Teams can adopt better models as they emerge while preserving their investments in the enterprise systems, knowledge, tools, and controls that make agents useful to their business. </p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Adopting a newer model is only the starting point. Foundry helps teams continuously improve their agents, using production traces to evaluate and refine instructions, skills, tools, and model choice against quality, latency, and cost. That’s the <strong>hill-climbing</strong> approach: observe, evaluate, optimize, validate, and repeat—with people in control</p>



<div class="wp-block-buttons is-content-justification-center is-layout-flex wp-container-core-buttons-is-layout-a89b3969 wp-block-buttons-is-layout-flex">
<div class="wp-block-button"><a class="wp-block-button__link wp-element-button" href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/products/ai-foundry" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Learn more about Microsoft Foundry</a></div>
</div>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Today, we’re advancing that foundation to help teams build and continuously improve agents that deliver business value:</p>



<ul class="wp-block-list">
<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Choose from a broader range of models</strong> as frontier AI advances.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Build voice experiences and support long-running work</strong> on the same agent foundation.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Equip agents with knowledge and tools</strong> that help them discover and apply the right capabilities.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Turn production insights into continuous improvement</strong> across agent quality, latency, and cost.</li>
</ul>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">These advances help teams spend less time assembling infrastructure and more time building.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">One customer putting these capabilities together is <a href="https://www.fashable.ai/">Fashable</a>, which uses Foundry’s models, agents, knowledge, and tools to help fashion brands turn trends and ideas into clothing designs, virtual try-on experiences, and campaign imagery. By connecting these workflows with the existing brands’ systems, Fashable reports cutting the time to develop new fashion products from months to weeks, with one customer reducing its budget for physical samples by 60%.</p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="choose-and-evaluate-frontier-ai-models-in-microsoft-foundry">Choose and evaluate frontier AI models in Microsoft Foundry</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Frontier AI is advancing rapidly across providers. New capabilities are arriving in days, not months, and this week captures that momentum: the full <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/gpt-6-astra-sol-and-luna-for-production-agents-in-microsoft-foundry/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"><strong>GPT-6 family from OpenAI</strong></a><strong>, including GPT-6 Sol and GPT-6 Luna, and </strong><a href="https://aka.ms/ClaudeOpus5.5" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"><strong>Claude Opus 5.5 from Anthropic</strong></a> are now available in Microsoft Foundry. What makes this moment important is not any single model. It is the pace and breadth of innovation happening across the frontier at the same time. </p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">For developers and enterprises, model choice is no longer a decision you make at the start of a project. It is becoming a continuous advantage. The best model for a coding agent today may not be the best model for research, customer service, or high-volume inference, and the right answer will keep changing as new models arrive. <strong>Microsoft Foundry is built for that reality.</strong> </p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Foundry brings together models from leading AI providers in an open platform where teams can evaluate them against their own data and workloads, compare tradeoffs across quality, latency, and cost, and put the right models into production without rebuilding their AI strategy around a single provider. The arrival of the GPT-6 family and Claude Opus 5.5 expands that choice, giving teams more models to evaluate for their specific workloads alongside the broader portfolio already available in Foundry.</p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="build-and-operate-voice-agents-with-foundry-agent-service">Build and operate voice agents with Foundry Agent Service</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Voice is rapidly becoming a major way people interact with AI agents. Yet many voice experiences are still built by layering speech onto agents designed for text, requiring separate speech, orchestration, monitoring, and deployment components. With <a href="https://aka.ms/VoicefirstagentsSept2026" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"><strong>voice agents in Foundry Agent Service</strong></a>, voice becomes a native part of the agent foundation—built, deployed, observed, evaluated, and optimized through the same platform, API, and SDK as the rest of your Foundry agents. </p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Voice agents, now in public preview, are a new agent type supporting both prompt and hosted agents. Choose from industry-leading voice models— GPT Realtime, Azure Realtime, MAI and more—to balance latency, reasoning depth, and cost for each scenario, or bring your own. Models, tools, and deployment paths are designed for spoken interaction from the start: expressive voices, natural turn-taking, and graceful recovery when someone interrupts, across 80+ languages and 140+ locales. Teams that want a distinct identity can fine-tune custom speech for domain vocabulary, create a custom voice, or add a photo or video avatar for unique brand identity. Developers can reuse the tools, enterprise knowledge, and governance configured for their other Foundry agents, then deploy to web channels, Microsoft Teams, Teams Phone, and Twilio-based inbound and outbound telephony experiences. </p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The developer workflow extends across the tools teams already use:</p>



<ul class="wp-block-list">
<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>AZD AI</strong> provides a repeatable, source-controlled workflow for configuring and deploying voice agents.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Foundry Toolkit in Visual Studio Code</strong> (<em>coming soon</em>) enables developers to create, run, speak with, and debug voice agents without leaving their development environment.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Voice-agent observability</strong> brings tracing, monitoring, transcripts, and voice-native evaluation to spoken interactions.</li>
</ul>



<blockquote class="wp-block-quote has-quote-default-font-size is-layout-flow wp-block-quote-is-layout-flow">
<p class="wp-block-paragraph">At Fashable, our mission is to make advanced AI accessible to brands and manufacturers of all sizes, regardless of their technical maturity. As we introduce new agentic capabilities, voice becomes an important part of that journey, enabling interactions that feel more natural, conversational, and accessible. People don&#8217;t think in prompts, they think in conversations, and using voice agents in Foundry with MAI-Voice helps bridge that gap.</p>
<cite>—Orlando Ribas Fernandes, CEO, Fashable</cite></blockquote>

<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="build-resilient-hosted-agents-for-long-running-tasks">Build resilient hosted agents for long-running tasks</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Real enterprise work does not always finish within a single request. A research task may span many sources, a report may require several tools and processing stages, and an agent may need to wait for external systems or human approval. <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/foundry/agents/concepts/long-running-agent-resilience"><strong>Long-running resilience in Foundry Agent Service</strong></a>, now in public preview, lets the work of hosted agents continue after a request disconnects and recover when its hosting process stops unexpectedly.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Microsoft Agent Framework complements these capabilities with framework-level workflow state and checkpointing. When a resilient background workflow is interrupted, Agent Framework can resume from its latest durable checkpoint while Foundry preserves the hosted response.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Microsoft Agent Framework also adds Agent Channel for messaging integrations, AG-UI support for interactive applications, CodeAct and Hyperlight containers for isolated code execution, and episodic procedural memory to reuse successful task patterns. <a href="https://aka.ms/MAFSept2026" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Read more</a> about the latest updates in Microsoft Agent Framework.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">To build and deploy these long-running agents, developers can use the Foundry Toolkit for Visual Studio Code, which supports hosted agents and Microsoft Agent Framework. The new <a href="https://aka.ms/DevpackSept2026" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"><strong>Foundry dev pack</strong></a> simplifies onboarding by bringing the Foundry developer toolchain together in one installer. </p>



<div class="wp-block-buttons is-content-justification-center is-layout-flex wp-container-core-buttons-is-layout-a89b3969 wp-block-buttons-is-layout-flex">
<div class="wp-block-button"><a class="wp-block-button__link wp-element-button" href="https://aka.ms/MAFSept2026" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Read more about the latest updates in Microsoft Agent Framework</a></div>
</div>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="give-agents-only-what-they-need-when-they-need-it">Give agents only what they need, when they need it </h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Enterprise agents don’t need every document, tool, and procedure in every interaction. Loading everything upfront adds latency and cost, and can make behavior harder to predict. Agents should be able to find and use the resources they need as the work unfolds.</p>



<ul class="wp-block-list">
<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/foundry/agents/how-to/tools/toolbox?pivots=vscode" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Toolboxes</a> </strong>give agents on-demand access to tools and reusable procedures, with opt-in discovery and tool-level guardrails. Teams can make capabilities available without loading them into every call. Toolboxes are already generally available for hosted agents and are now expanding to prompt agents in public preview.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/foundry/agents/how-to/tools/tool-search?pivots=python" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Tool search</a> in Toolboxes</strong>, now generally available, lets agents find the right tool without loading the full catalog of tool definitions upfront. In an <a href="https://commandline.microsoft.com/tool-search-toolboxes-foundry/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">internal evaluation</a> using a <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.01763" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">public benchmark</a> of more than 44,000 tools and 7,000 queries, tool search reduced input-token consumption by more than 60% for a 50-tool toolbox and by over 97% for a 1,000-tool toolbox. These results are compared with a prompt-cached baseline that loads the full tool catalog upfront.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/foundry/agents/how-to/tools/agent-to-agent?pivots=python" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"><strong>Agent-to-Agent (A2A)</strong></a>, now generally available, lets agents call other agents, including other Foundry agents, through an open, standardized protocol. Teams can connect agents without building custom point-to-point integrations, while centralized credentials and policy enforcement help maintain security and governance as those systems grow. </li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong><a href="https://devblogs.microsoft.com/foundry/from-chatbots-to-automated-assistants-routines-in-microsoft-foundry-are-now-generally-available/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Routines</a> in Foundry Agent Service</strong>, now generally available, let teams run automated agents on a schedule, after a delay, or in response to an event. Teams no longer need to build their own schedulers, queues, and identity plumbing just to start an agent at the right time.</li>
</ul>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Together, these capabilities help teams reduce unnecessary context and custom infrastructure, with more control over what agents can access and when they run. </p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="turn-production-evidence-into-continuous-improvement">Turn production evidence into continuous improvement</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Getting an agent into production is just the beginning. As real users put it to work, teams need to understand what’s driving quality, latency, and cost, and which changes will improve performance without introducing regressions. That has often meant piecing together traces, dashboards, and ad hoc tests. Foundry brings those steps into a continuous improvement loop, helping teams turn production insights into measurable improvements: observe, understand, evaluate, optimize, validate, and repeat. </p>


<figure data-wp-context="{&quot;imageId&quot;:&quot;6ac7895da7728&quot;}" data-wp-interactive="core/image" data-wp-key="6ac7895da7728" class="wp-block-image size-full wp-lightbox-container"><img decoding="async" data-wp-class--hide="state.isContentHidden" data-wp-class--show="state.isContentVisible" data-wp-init="callbacks.setButtonStyles" data-wp-on--click="actions.showLightbox" data-wp-on--load="callbacks.setButtonStyles" data-wp-on-window--resize="callbacks.setButtonStyles" src="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/foundry-agent-loop-v2.webp" alt="Circular diagram showing a continuous agent improvement loop: observe traces and signals; understand quality, latency, and cost; evaluate changes; optimize the agent; and validate that it&rsquo;s better. Arrows connect the five stages in a repeating cycle." class="wp-image-54162 webp-format" srcset="" data-orig-src="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/foundry-agent-loop-v2.webp"><button

<ul class="wp-block-list">
<li class="wp-block-list-item"><a href="https://aka.ms/ObservabilitySept2026" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">Insights in Foundry</a>, now in public preview, helps teams understand what is happening in production. It analyzes production traces to surface recurring issues and ones teams didn&#8217;t know to look for. For each issue, it gathers the supporting evidence, points to the likely cause, and recommends what to do next, so teams know where to focus before a small problem becomes a widespread one. </li>
</ul>


<figure data-wp-context="{&quot;imageId&quot;:&quot;6ac7895da84ff&quot;}" data-wp-interactive="core/image" data-wp-key="6ac7895da84ff" class="wp-block-image size-large wp-lightbox-container"><img decoding="async" data-wp-class--hide="state.isContentHidden" data-wp-class--show="state.isContentVisible" data-wp-init="callbacks.setButtonStyles" data-wp-on--click="actions.showLightbox" data-wp-on--load="callbacks.setButtonStyles" data-wp-on-window--resize="callbacks.setButtonStyles" src="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/Insights-in-Microsoft-Foundry-1024x527.webp" alt="Insights in Foundry surfaces recurring agent issues, likely causes, supporting traces, and recommended actions." class="wp-image-54128 webp-format" srcset="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/Insights-in-Microsoft-Foundry-1024x527.webp 1024w, https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/Insights-in-Microsoft-Foundry-300x154.webp 300w, https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/Insights-in-Microsoft-Foundry-768x395.webp 768w, https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/Insights-in-Microsoft-Foundry-1536x790.webp 1536w, https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/Insights-in-Microsoft-Foundry.webp 2000w" data-orig-src="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/Insights-in-Microsoft-Foundry-1024x527.webp"><button

<ul class="wp-block-list">
<li class="wp-block-list-item"><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/foundry/concepts/evaluation-evaluators/rubric-evaluators" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Rubric evaluator</a>, generally available later this month, helps teams define what good looks like once they know what to fix. It turns an agent&#8217;s prompts, traces, and requirements into clear success criteria that can be measured consistently.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/foundry/observability/how-to/traces-to-dataset?tabs=python" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Synthetic and traces</a> to dataset generation, generally available later this month, builds the evaluation data to match those criteria. Traces to dataset generation draws on real production traffic, so tests reflect the scenarios customers actually encounter, including the ones Foundry insights flagged.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/foundry/agents/concepts/agent-optimizer-overview" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">Agent optimizer</a> in Foundry Agent Service, generally available later this month, puts those evaluations, datasets, and constraints to work. It tests changes across instructions, tools, skills, and model choice, and shows which changes actually improve results before anything ships.</li>
</ul>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Together, these capabilities close the loop from production back to production. The improved agent returns to real traffic, where Foundry insights keep watching for what comes next, and each pass builds on the last so agents keep getting better the longer they run.</p>



<blockquote class="wp-block-quote has-quote-default-font-size is-layout-flow wp-block-quote-is-layout-flow">
<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Agent optimizer in Foundry Agent Service turns evaluation insights into actionable datasets and optimization, reducing the manual effort of finding and fixing performance issues. That systematic loop aligns directly with NTT DATA&#8217;s AgentOps with Harness approach—a core element of our Smart AI Agent® concept—together moving agents from proof of concept to trusted production at scale.</p>
<cite>—Takashi Okamoto, AI Technology Strategist, Global AI Office, NTT DATA Group Corporation</cite></blockquote>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="govern-ai-agents-with-identity-lifecycle-and-network-controls">Govern AI agents with identity, lifecycle, and network controls</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">As agents take on more work, organizations need to manage them as enterprise assets, with identities, owners, policies, lifecycle controls, and evidence of what they did.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Foundry agents now honor governance operations originating in Microsoft Entra and Agent 365. </strong>Actions such as blocking, disabling, deleting, restoring, or assigning a new owner are enforced by the Foundry runtime rather than recorded only in a directory.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><a href="https://aka.ms/EgresscontrolsSept2026">Network egress controls</a> govern the outbound destinations a hosted agent can access. Teams can define allow and deny rules, modify outbound headers, or redirect requests before traffic leaves the Foundry-managed sandbox. An audit mode enables teams to evaluate policies before enforcement, with each decision emitted to Azure Application insights.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Coming soon in October, Microsoft Foundry is expanding its integration with Azure API Management with preview support for the new <strong>AI Gateway tier</strong>, helping organizations bring centralized governance into the Foundry developer experience. With this integration, organizations can adopt a <strong>hub-and-spoke approach</strong> where platform teams centrally govern model access through AI Gateway and make approved models available to developers through <strong>Admin Connected Models</strong>. Developers can then use these governed models across familiar Foundry experiences for model discovery, experimentation, agent development, and evaluation. Developers will also be able to connect to models across other resources or outside of Foundry directly from the playground. Existing Foundry integration with traditional Azure API Management tiers will remain supported.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Governing agents as enterprise assets also means proving they meet the organization&#8217;s safety and policy requirements. The new <a href="https://aka.ms/ASSERT-ACS-Clarity-Sept2026" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">open-source run-assert-eval Skill</a> brings that verification into a single workflow.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">It runs three of Microsoft&#8217;s open-source tools in sequence: </p>



<ol start="1" class="wp-block-list">
<li class="wp-block-list-item"><a href="https://github.com/microsoft/clarity-agent" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">Clarity</a> surfaces risks developers may have missed. </li>
</ol>



<ol start="2" class="wp-block-list">
<li class="wp-block-list-item"><a href="https://github.com/microsoft/ASSERT" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">ASSERT</a> turns requirements into measurable evaluations. </li>
</ol>



<ol start="3" class="wp-block-list">
<li class="wp-block-list-item"><a href="https://github.com/microsoft/agent-governance-toolkit/tree/main/policy-engine" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">Agent Control Specification (ACS)</a> applies targeted runtime controls where an agent falls short.  </li>
</ol>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The Skill then re-runs the evaluation to confirm the agent improved without restricting legitimate behavior, giving teams evidence that each policy is working as intended. </p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="get-started-with-microsoft-foundry">Get started with Microsoft Foundry</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The latest capabilities in Microsoft Foundry give developers a flexible path from a simple prompt to a production agent, without requiring teams to assemble disconnected development tools, runtimes, gateways, schedulers, and governance controls. </p>



<ul class="wp-block-list">
<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Start building:</strong> Head to <a href="https://ai.azure.com/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">ai.azure.com</a> to build your next agent in Microsoft Foundry. Choose a model, define the agent’s instructions, and connect the knowledge and tools it needs. </li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Learn with the community:</strong> Join <a href="https://aka.ms/model-mondays" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">Model Mondays</a> and <a href="https://aka.ms/model-mondays/discord" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">Foundry Fridays</a> for demos and practical guidance. Next up: <a href="https://discord.com/events/1113626258182504448/1542207383823196290/1553096245248000000" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">Agent Optimizer and insights in Foundry Session</a></li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Join us at Microsoft Ignite:</strong> Visit <a href="https://ignite.microsoft.com/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">Microsoft Ignite</a> to explore sessions on AI and agents and opportunities to connect with experts.  </li>
</ul>

		<title>Your architecture diagram is not your resilience</title>
		<link>https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/your-architecture-diagram-is-not-your-resilience/</link>
					<comments>https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/your-architecture-diagram-is-not-your-resilience/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Mark Russinovich, Adam Bogobowicz and Molina Sharma]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 23 Sep 2026 16:00:00 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Analytics]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[DevOps]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Management and governance]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Advancing reliability]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Azure Essentials]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Azure Well-Architected Framework]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/?p=53956</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>For years, resilience was something you set up once. That kept the lights on, but it treated resilience as a project with an end date rather than a property you maintain.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/your-architecture-diagram-is-not-your-resilience/">Your architecture diagram is not your resilience</a> appeared first on <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog">Microsoft Azure Blog</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[
<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>This first article in our resilience series draws on a conversation with Mark Russinovich about how resilience is changing in the AI era and what it takes to continuously validate it at scale.</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">What surprises me most about resilience failures is how ordinary the drift is. A workload is deployed across availability zones, but a health probe still points to a single dependency. A database supports failover, but the application&#8217;s connection string is pinned to one region. Nothing looks broken. The architecture diagram still shows a resilient design even as the operational reality underneath it changes.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">For years, resilience was something you set up once: configure disaster recovery, write a runbook, run the occasional failover test. That kept the lights on, but it treated resilience as a project with an end date rather than a property you maintain.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">So, when an availability zone or a region has a bad day, the question is not whether a recovery plan exists on paper. It is whether resilience is still true today, and whether the team can prove it.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The dependency that breaks a workload is also changing. On Microsoft&#8217;s FY26 Q4 earnings call, Satya Nadella put it plainly: &#8220;For whatever reason, if a given model goes away, then you can&#8217;t be left high and dry. You need to be able to still continue your cyber operations.&#8221;</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Traditional disaster recovery planning assumes the critical dependency is infrastructure. Increasingly it is an AI model, an inference endpoint, a retrieval pipeline, or a service operating under capacity constraints. A workload can be perfectly healthy from an infrastructure perspective and still fail its users because that dependency is unavailable, throttled, or economically impractical to run. That dependency rarely appears on the diagram at all.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">There is a second shift underneath the first. An architecture diagram assumes a human drew it and a human will read it. Both of those assumptions are ending, and the dependencies it describes are no longer all deterministic. That changes what it means to know your estate is resilient.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">This is the first in a series on how we are helping customers move to resilience that is designed in, measured as the estate changes, and improved over time. We have written before about how to design a resilient workload. This is a different problem: knowing whether hundreds of workloads still match their design today and being able to prove it. It is where a large share of our roadmap investment is now going.</p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="why-resilience-drifts"><strong>Why resilience drifts</strong></h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Resilience has always been a shared responsibility. We provide the availability zones, a secondary region of choice, and the replication primitives needed to support resiliency, and those do not drift. What drifts is the other half of the bargain: whether a given workload still uses them the way it was designed to, after a year of changes nobody flagged as risky. Change is where this concentrates. Across the industry, roughly 70 percent of cloud outages are related to change in some way—not dramatic failures, but ordinary modifications whose blast radius nobody re-evaluated.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">That is why change discipline matters as much as design. Internally, a change rolls out to a canary region first, then a pilot region, with bake times where health signals are watched before it goes any further. That practice came out of an incident of our own, and it is the same discipline the <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/well-architected/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Well-Architected Framework</a> describes as safe deployment practices.</p>



<div class="wp-block-buttons is-content-justification-center is-layout-flex wp-container-core-buttons-is-layout-a89b3969 wp-block-buttons-is-layout-flex">
<div class="wp-block-button"><a class="wp-block-button__link wp-element-button" href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/well-architected/">Learn more about the Azure Well-Architected Framework</a></div>
</div>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Disaster recovery is reactive by design. Teams set recovery objectives when a project ships, stand up replication, and then move on, with little ongoing visibility into whether those objectives still hold as the workload changes. Resilience is designed once and rarely revisited. Across a growing estate of availability zones and regions, the distance between the resilience that was designed and the resilience that actually exists widens quietly, and it usually surfaces only during an incident.</p>



<figure class="wp-block-bloginabox-theme-ump-embed wp-block-msx-ump-embed" class="wp-block-bloginabox-theme-ump-embed" data-bi-dnt="true" role="presentation">
	<div class="wp-block-embed__wrapper">

<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>We learned this the hard way. Mark tells the story of the storage change that came close to taking Azure down, and what it permanently changed about how we deploy.</em></p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="what-a-diagram-can-t-tell-you"><strong>What a diagram can&#8217;t tell you</strong></h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">A diagram is a claim about a system, made once, by someone reasoning about the system as they believed it to be. It is useful, and it is not evidence. There are four things it structurally cannot tell you.</p>



<ol class="wp-block-list">
<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Whether the goal is being met right now.</strong> A diagram has no timestamp. Health modeling does: described in the Well-Architected Framework and now available through health models in <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/products/monitor" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Azure Monitor</a>, it represents an application as a hierarchy of its components and the signals underneath them, so health is expressed in terms the business recognizes rather than as a wall of resource-level metrics. Paired with service level indicators, it answers the only question that matters during an incident: is this application meeting its objective right now? The standard that matters is not our own. A service is only healthy if the customer thinks it is healthy—we can believe it is fine, but if the customer is not seeing a healthy service, we have a problem.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>What &#8220;resilient&#8221; even means for this application.</strong> On a diagram, resilient is an adjective. A resiliency goal makes it a threshold you either meet or miss and defining it at the level of the application rather than resource by resource is what makes the answer meaningful.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Whether the failover path actually works.</strong> Every diagram draws the arrow. Only a test proves it.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>The resources nobody drew.</strong> A diagram shows what someone remembered. Generated Infrastructure-as-Code covers every resource in the application. The difference between those two sets is where drift begins—and increasingly the reader on the other end is an agent rather than a person, working from the outcome you asked for instead of the picture you drew.</li>
</ol>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">This is how we run Azure. Rather than asking each team to declare what healthy means for their service, we standardized on service level indicators, then applied machine learning to observed behavior so that healthy is defined by what the service actually does rather than by what someone assumed it would do.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">This is the part I find most interesting. We wrote our documentation, our schemas, and our templates for people. Increasingly the thing reading them is an agent, working from the outcome you asked for rather than the picture you drew, and generating the resources itself. When the author and the reader are both machines, a diagram is no longer even the medium the decision is made in.</p>



<figure class="wp-block-bloginabox-theme-ump-embed wp-block-msx-ump-embed" class="wp-block-bloginabox-theme-ump-embed" data-bi-dnt="true" role="presentation">
	<div class="wp-block-embed__wrapper">

<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>Mark on why we break our own services on purpose, what a game day actually tests, and why every failure mode we simulate is one that has really happened.</em></p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="when-the-dependency-is-probabilistic"><strong>When the dependency is probabilistic</strong></h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">A model that disappears is the obvious risk. The subtler one is a model that answers, differently each time. It is a newer source of drift, and it does not behave like the old ones. Ask the same question of a model twice and you can get two different answers. That makes correctness harder to define, and it makes change harder to reason about: if you change the prompt, change the model, or change the harness and the skills around it, you have changed the software, and it deserves the same discipline as any other change.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Most teams smoke-test instead. It looks fine, so it ships. Evaluation is the step that gets skipped, and it is the one that matters: measuring the system against what you actually consider valuable, not simply whether it responded.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The first question is whether the system needs to be probabilistic at all. Stay as deterministic as you can and use AI where it earns its place, not everywhere. And where you can wrap a non-deterministic system in a deterministic check, do it. If an agent is only supposed to update dependency versions, have a second system verify that versions are the only thing that changed. Where a deterministic check is not possible, use adversarial review: a second agent whose whole job is to find what is wrong with the first one’s work.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">And accountability does not transfer. When someone deploys an agent, someone remains answerable for what it does.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">That applies to us as well. Our own incident triage system uses language models to answer which service is responsible for an incident—work that used to mean waking people up to argue over logs. It is genuinely faster, and it is still trust but verify.</p>



<figure class="wp-block-bloginabox-theme-ump-embed wp-block-msx-ump-embed" class="wp-block-bloginabox-theme-ump-embed" data-bi-dnt="true" role="presentation">
	<div class="wp-block-embed__wrapper">

<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>Mark on why guardrails around AI should be deterministic wherever possible, and why the agent is never the one accountable.</em></p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="what-this-looks-like-in-practice"><strong>What this looks like in practice</strong></h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">That discipline has to hold wherever a workload runs, and the foundations are familiar. The reliability guidance in the Well-Architected Framework already says to design resilience in from the start, and the <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/cloud-adoption-framework/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Cloud Adoption Framework</a> describes the operating half: carrying that intent into how the estate is actually run and reviewed month after month. Where an estate spans global, national, and sovereign or regulated environments, the word resilient has to mean the same thing in each rather than being redefined at every boundary; our guidance on reliability and sovereignty covers where those constraints intersect.</p>



<ul class="wp-block-list">
<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Your diagram shows three zones. It does not show that the health probe behind the load balancer resolves to one of them.</strong> Availability zones protect a workload from datacenter-level failures within a region, but only if compute, storage, and data tiers are genuinely spread across them.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Plan region resiliency for disaster recovery.</strong> Set explicit recovery objectives, an RTO and RPO, for each workload. Availability zones give you high availability inside a region, while a secondary region of choice provides a failover location when an entire region is affected. They answer different risks, and a resilient design is deliberate about both. In a regulated estate the choice narrows further, because a recovery region has to sit inside the same jurisdiction as the workload it protects.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Decide how much resilience is worth buying.</strong> Resilience is a cost decision as much as a design one. An application carrying 100 million dollars of revenue on a single day justifies an active-active topology across regions for that day; the same application may run in a single region with active-passive failover for the rest of the year. The right answer is deliberate, not maximal.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Know your blast radius.</strong> Understand what each workload depends on and where its hidden single points of failure are, then keep that picture current as the application changes. Internally we do this as reliability threat modeling: the same discipline as security threat modeling, asking of each component what would happen if it failed, and what we would do about it.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Check the dependencies your recovery path itself relies on.</strong> A workload can be replicated correctly and still be unrecoverable: if its encryption keys live only in the primary region, they are gone precisely when a region outage means you need them. Recovery paths have dependencies too, and they are rarely on the diagram.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Your diagram probably has no box for the AI dependency your application now relies on.</strong> Design for your application, workload, AI model, and service dependencies, not just infrastructure. Plan for graceful degradation and fallback so that if a critical dependency is deprecated, throttled, unavailable, or capacity-constrained, the workload continues to operate through an alternative path rather than failing outright.</li>
</ul>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">This is not theoretical. Carne Group, one of Europe’s largest independent third-party asset managers with one trillion dollars under management, rebuilt its estate on Azure with infrastructure-as-code landing zones precisely so resilience would be reproducible rather than remembered. Because the definition lives in code, their team can stand up a duplicate site in another region and, as Carne Group’s global technology lead Stéphane Bebrone puts it, “even in the event of a worst-case scenario, we could be back up and running more or less in the same day.” They are working toward an active-passive topology across regions and plan to use <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/products/chaos-studio" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Chaos Studio</a> to verify those failover paths on a schedule rather than on an incident. Under DORA, they have to be able to prove it, not assert it.</p>



<div class="wp-block-buttons is-content-justification-center is-layout-flex wp-container-core-buttons-is-layout-a89b3969 wp-block-buttons-is-layout-flex">
<div class="wp-block-button"><a class="wp-block-button__link wp-element-button" href="https://www.microsoft.com/en/customers/story/26204-carne-group-azure-site-recovery" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Learn more about how Carne Group used Azure Site Recovery and Backup</a></div>
</div>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="closing-the-gap-between-intent-and-reality"><strong>Closing the gap between intent and reality</strong></h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">An architecture diagram is a statement of intent, not proof. It may show zone redundancy, regional failover, and protected dependencies, but only a test can determine whether those assumptions still hold. Assembling the underlying data into a clear view of application resiliency takes real effort and expertise. That is the customer&#8217;s half of the shared responsibility, and it is the gap <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/products/infrastructure-resiliency-manager" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Azure Infrastructure Resiliency Manager</a> is built to help close. In public preview, its agentic-first experience helps teams start resilient, get resilient, and stay resilient.</p>



<ul class="wp-block-list">
<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Start resilient.</strong> Define what resilient means for an application, set explicit resiliency goals, and use the Resiliency Agent to generate resiliency-aware Infrastructure-as-Code up front, so a new workload starts resilient instead of being corrected months later. Service Groups help teams represent an application as a logical group of Azure resources in the portal, making it easier to manage resiliency posture at the application level rather than resource by resource.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Get resilient.</strong> See where the estate stands against the intended design, identify resources that were never zone resilient or stopped being so after a change, and prioritize the gaps that matter first. Rather than correlating findings across several tools, teams get recommendations and generated Infrastructure-as-Code for supported fixes, so remediation can move through a pull request instead of becoming a separate project.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Stay resilient.</strong> Validate the assumptions behind the recovery path before a real outage does it for you. For workloads where the customer manages the compute, such as virtual machines, a zone-down drill can simulate the loss of an availability zone and show what actually happens to the application. For other services, teams can use failover validation, product-specific recovery capabilities, and fault injection through Azure Chaos Studio to test the right failure modes.</li>
</ul>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">We will be candid about the gaps too. Consistent, self-service resiliency assessment across every workload and environment is not finished work, and saying so matters. <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/solutions/resiliency/?msockid=23fc7cc163eb6df416706b8962896c7b" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Resiliency </a>improves when teams can see the gaps, measure them, and systematically close them over time. It is also work that never quite ends. As services harden the failure rate drops, but the failures that remain get rarer and stranger; you approach perfection asymptotically without ever arriving.</p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="the-bottom-line-a-diagram-is-not-proof"><strong>The bottom line: a diagram is not proof</strong></h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Resilience is not a project you finish; it is a posture you maintain. The work is to design it in from the first architecture decision, then keep proving it as the estate changes, so drift is caught by a test rather than by an incident. All of it is anchored in the <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/solutions/azure-essentials" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Azure Essentials</a> frameworks, the Well-Architected Framework, and the Cloud Adoption Framework, so that resilient and sovereign carry the same meaning across every environment instead of being reinvented team by team.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">None of this is a new framework. A diagram tells you what you intended; only a test tells you what you have. That was true when people drew the diagrams and read them. It matters more now that neither is reliably the case, and that some of what you depend on answers differently every time you ask. If resilience cannot be tested, it cannot be trusted.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">In the next part of this series, we will look at how to measure that posture at scale.</p>



<figure class="wp-block-bloginabox-theme-ump-embed wp-block-msx-ump-embed" class="wp-block-bloginabox-theme-ump-embed" data-bi-dnt="true" role="presentation">
	<div class="wp-block-embed__wrapper">

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Designing agent-first platforms: What changes when agents do the work</title>
		<link>https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/designing-agent-first-platforms-what-changes-when-agents-do-the-work/</link>
					<comments>https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/designing-agent-first-platforms-what-changes-when-agents-do-the-work/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Mike Hulme]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 23 Sep 2026 15:00:00 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[AI + machine learning]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Containers]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[DevOps]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/?p=53970</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>The organizations pulling ahead are not simply adding AI to what they already have. They are designing for a different kind of software.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/designing-agent-first-platforms-what-changes-when-agents-do-the-work/">Designing agent-first platforms: What changes when agents do the work</a> appeared first on <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog">Microsoft Azure Blog</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[
<p class="wp-block-paragraph">For decades, applications have been designed to wait. A user clicks, a request arrives, code runs, a response goes back. And we got very good at this. We learned to forecast traffic, scale on demand, wrap everything in enterprise controls, and run it all with the operational discipline that keeps critical business systems available around the clock.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">That model is being turned on its head, and being asked to serve apps that continuously act, and wait for no one.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">What changed is not the infrastructure underneath, but the software being written on top of it. The work we used to capture as deterministic code, where every branch was defined in advance and every step was known before the first line ran, is now being rewritten as multi-agent applications that work out the steps at runtime. A developer used to encode the path, and now a developer describes the outcome and lets a set of agents reason their way toward it.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Agents work differently. Given an outcome, an agent reasons through the problem, breaks it into steps, writes code to solve it, runs that code, looks at the result, and goes again. The work happens in a loop that no human is standing inside. That change puts pressure on assumptions our platforms were built on, because an application that responds and an agent that acts need very different things underneath them.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The organizations pulling ahead right now are the ones who understood this early. They are not simply adding AI to what they already have. They are designing for a different kind of software.</p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="what-it-takes-to-run-an-agent-you-can-depend-on">What it takes to run an agent you can depend on</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Getting an agent working is no longer the hard part. A team can connect a capable model to a few tools, ground it in company data, and have something genuinely useful inside a week. That is real progress, and it is why so many organizations now have a pilot that impressed the room.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The distance between that pilot and an agent the business depends on around the clock is where the real challenge is.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">An agent operating continuously on behalf of a company gets held to the same bar as everything else in production. It needs its own identity, with permissions scoped to what it is allowed to see. It needs to be watched while it works, so a team can trace every step it took, evaluate whether the outcome was right, and catch the drift that shows up quietly weeks after launch. It needs guardrails that are enforced at runtime. And it has to clear the same security and compliance standards as the rest of the estate. No one is going to grant an agent an exemption.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">None of that is work a team should be doing for itself. That is the job of an agent platform. <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/products/ai-foundry" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Microsoft Foundry</a> is where agents are built, grounded in enterprise knowledge, given a first-class identity through <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/entra/agent-id/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Entra Agent ID</a>, and traced and evaluated once they are live. The Foundry Control Plane governs the agent, but it does not dictate where the agent&#8217;s work actually runs. That is a separate decision, and a separate layer.</p>



<div class="wp-block-buttons is-content-justification-center is-layout-flex wp-container-core-buttons-is-layout-a89b3969 wp-block-buttons-is-layout-flex">
<div class="wp-block-button"><a class="wp-block-button__link wp-element-button" href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/products/ai-foundry" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Get started with Microsoft Foundry</a></div>
</div>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="where-the-work-actually-runs">Where the work actually runs</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The moment an agent stops answering questions and starts completing tasks, it has to execute code. It clones a repository. It installs a package. It runs an analysis against live data and calls into a system of record. That execution needs a runtime environment. In most cases, it simply inherits the one the host application is already running on, because that is the path of least resistance.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Run the agent on shared infrastructure and every workload inherits the blast radius of every other one. Give it broad access so it can be useful and you have handed an autonomous process far more reach than you intended. Lock it down until it is safe and the agent can no longer do the job you built it for. Teams get stuck in this trade-off, and it is where most promising agents usually stall before they reach production.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The answer is not to limit what the agent can do. It is to give it a dedicated environment, with its own identity and guardrails for execution.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/container-apps/sandboxes-get-started" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Azure Container Apps Sandboxes</a> provides exactly that. Every agent execution gets a fully isolated environment of its own, created in seconds and gone when the work is finished. It runs as an identity you control, can access only the systems you have allowed it to reach, and never stores the credentials it uses to get there. And when a task spans hours or days, the environment can be paused and resumed with its full working context intact, so the agent picks up precisely where it left off.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">There is real engineering behind this. Each environment runs inside its own hardware-isolated microVM, which is what makes strong separation and sub-second startup possible at the same time. But the mechanism is not the point. Isolation is built into the runtime rather than wrapped around it, so teams stop choosing between a capable agent and a controlled one.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Nothing disappears into the sandbox. The agent is still governed by Foundry, so what it sent into the sandbox and what came back stay on the record with every other step it took.</p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="the-architecture-pattern-emerging-across-the-enterprise">The architecture pattern emerging across the enterprise</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Put the two together and a clear design pattern appears, one we are now seeing repeatedly across industries.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Build and govern the agent on Foundry. Extend its execution into an isolated sandbox on <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/products/container-apps" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Azure Container Apps</a>. The agent keeps its identity, its permissions, and its oversight. The agent does not change when its work moves. Only the ground it runs on does. The work it performs happens in an environment that is purpose-built for agent tasks with ultra-fast executions, scoped to what it needs, and dissolved afterward.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">This is what allows an organization to move from a handful of supervised agents to thousands running concurrently, without asking security and platform teams to accept risk they should never be asked to accept.</p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="what-this-looks-like-in-practice">What this looks like in practice</h2>



<ul class="wp-block-list">
<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Regulated client work, delivered at scale.</strong> Digital Gateway Powered by Claude brings Claude&#8217;s capabilities into KPMG&#8217;s connected tax platform on Microsoft Azure, giving professionals a secure, trusted environment to work alongside AI. Within the platform, DG Cowork serves as the AI-powered workspace where professionals can analyze information, generate content, and advance complex work. Because client data must remain separated by engagement, the platform was built with engagement-specific workspaces and controls at its foundation. DG Cowork is designed for a global scale, with more than 30,000 Azure Container Apps Sandboxes running concurrently.</li>
</ul>



<ul class="wp-block-list">
<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Delivering answers to the questions nobody had time to ask.</strong> Cognite serves industrial operators who face a common challenge: their most valuable questions—like which assets are underperforming and why— are often too time-consuming to answer. Because traditional analysis took days, these investigations were rarely commissioned, leaving teams to rely on experience alone. Cognite Atlas AI changes that. Each agent gets its own governed workspace grounded in live customer data in Cognite Data Fusion, turning days-long investigations into traceable answers delivered in minutes. And for more complex problems, agents can pause and resume investigations without losing context, allowing them to work through a problem over time much like an engineer would.</li>
</ul>



<blockquote class="wp-block-quote is-style-default has-quote-default-font-size is-layout-flow wp-block-quote-is-layout-flow">
<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Atlas AI agents are powered by Azure OpenAI models in Microsoft Foundry, but to handle complex work in Cognite Data Fusion they needed to execute arbitrary code and work with files directly &#8211; which meant sandboxes that truly isolate each user&#8217;s agent, environment, and data. With Azure Container Apps Sandboxes we had a prototype running in hours and were testing with customers within weeks, because Azure handles the hard part: per-user isolation, egress policies, sub-second execution. For our industrial customers, that&#8217;s the difference between a days-long manual investigation into &#8220;which wells are underperforming and why?&#8221; and a cited draft in minutes.</p>
<cite> —Christian Flasshoff, Architect, Atlas AI, Cognite</cite></blockquote>



<ul class="wp-block-list">
<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>A safe place for every learner. </strong>The Department for Education in South Australia runs EdChat so students can learn by writing code and exploring data alongside AI, across 60,000 students and more than 40,000 staff. That model only works if every student gets an environment of their own, with clear guardrails, and can come back later to find their work exactly as they left it. Building that in house meant owning the machinery behind it. The department estimates that moving to Azure Container Apps Sandboxes lets them retire close to 50,000 lines of code written to manage custom code interpreter state themselves. It is a per-user execution model at the scale of a school system, and a maintenance burden the department no longer has to carry.</li>
</ul>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Three very different organizations. One common story. None of them were held back by what the models could do. They were held back by not having anywhere to run the work at their scale, against their real data, with economics that made sense.</p>



<div class="wp-block-buttons is-content-justification-center is-layout-flex wp-container-core-buttons-is-layout-a89b3969 wp-block-buttons-is-layout-flex">
<div class="wp-block-button"><a class="wp-block-button__link wp-element-button" href="https://aka.ms/aca/sandbox/launch-blog" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Learn more about Azure Container Apps Sandboxes</a></div>
</div>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="we-built-this-because-we-needed-it">We built this because we needed it</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">More than a million sandboxes per day run in production across Microsoft, powering <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/products/github/Copilot" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">GitHub Copilot</a>, <a href="https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/microsoft-365-copilot/microsoft-copilot-studio" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Copilot Studio</a>, <a href="https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/business/ai-machine-learning/microsoft-security-copilot" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Security Copilot</a>, <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/products/ai-foundry/agent-service" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Foundry Agent Service</a>, <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/products/sre-agent" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Azure SRE Agent</a>, and other Azure services.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Every one of those sandboxes has someone on the other end of it. A developer expecting a pull request to come back finished. An analyst chasing an alert before the shift ends. A support team working down a queue that has to be empty by morning. Their day moves at the speed this layer runs, and it either holds or it doesn&#8217;t. That is a sterner test than anything we could design internally, and it is the one this infrastructure has been passing at scale for a long while now.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">One architecture sits beneath both our developer tools and our AI platform. That is the same architecture we are making generally available to you.</p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="the-decision-in-front-of-you">The decision in front of you</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Every major platform shift eventually comes down to a design choice made early, by people who could see where things were heading.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The teams who will scale agents successfully over the next few years are making that choice now. They are separating the platform that governs the agent from the environment that runs its work, and they are treating that separation as a foundational decision rather than something to retrofit once a pilot succeeds.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The agents are ready. The question worth asking is whether your platform is designed for software that acts.</p>



<div class="is-style-inline-centered wp-block-bloginabox-theme-promotional">
	
<div class="promotional promotional--has-media promotional--media-right">
	<div class="promotional__wrapper">

			<div class="promotional__content">

<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Learn more about designing agent-first platforms with Azure.</p>

</div>
